<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Mike Pearson" redirects here. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/Les_Pearson" title="Les Pearson">Les Pearson</a> (rugby player), or <a href="/wiki/Les_Pearson_(baseball)" title="Les Pearson (baseball)">Les Pearson (baseball)</a>.</div>

<p><b>Lester Bowles</b> "<b>Mike</b>" <b>Pearson</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/King%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" title="King&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">PC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Member_of_the_Order_of_Merit" class="mw-redirect" title="Member of the Order of Merit">OM</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">CC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_British_Empire" title="Order of the British Empire">OBE</a></span> (23 April 1897&#160;– 27 December 1972) was a Canadian politician, diplomat, statesman, and scholar who served as the 14th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a> from 1963 to 1968.
</p><p>Born in <a href="/wiki/Newtonbrook" title="Newtonbrook">Newtonbrook</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ontario" title="Ontario">Ontario</a> (now part of <a href="/wiki/Toronto" title="Toronto">Toronto</a>), Pearson pursued a career in the <a href="/wiki/Global_Affairs_Canada" title="Global Affairs Canada">Department of External Affairs</a>. He served as <a href="/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_Canada_to_the_United_States" title="List of ambassadors of Canada to the United States">Canadian ambassador to the United States</a> from 1944 to 1946 and <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs_(Canada)" title="Minister of Foreign Affairs (Canada)">secretary of state for external affairs</a> from 1948 to 1957 under <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a> Prime Ministers <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">William Lyon Mackenzie King</a> and <a href="/wiki/Louis_St._Laurent" title="Louis St. Laurent">Louis St. Laurent</a>. He narrowly lost the bid to become <a href="/wiki/Secretary-General_of_the_United_Nations" title="Secretary-General of the United Nations">secretary-general of the United Nations</a> in <a href="/wiki/1953_United_Nations_Secretary-General_selection" title="1953 United Nations Secretary-General selection">1953</a>. However, he won the <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a> in 1957 for organizing the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Emergency_Force" title="United Nations Emergency Force">United Nations Emergency Force</a> to resolve the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal_Crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Suez Canal Crisis">Suez Canal Crisis</a>, which earned him attention worldwide. After the Liberals' defeat in the <a href="/wiki/1957_Canadian_federal_election" title="1957 Canadian federal election">1957 federal election</a>, Pearson easily won the <a href="/wiki/1958_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1958 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">leadership of the Liberal Party in 1958</a>. Pearson suffered two consecutive defeats by <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservative</a> Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/John_Diefenbaker" title="John Diefenbaker">John Diefenbaker</a> in <a href="/wiki/1958_Canadian_federal_election" title="1958 Canadian federal election">1958</a> and <a href="/wiki/1962_Canadian_federal_election" title="1962 Canadian federal election">1962</a>, only to successfully challenge him for a third time in the <a href="/wiki/1963_Canadian_federal_election" title="1963 Canadian federal election">1963 federal election</a>. Pearson would win re-election in <a href="/wiki/1965_Canadian_federal_election" title="1965 Canadian federal election">1965</a>.
</p><p>Pearson ran two back-to-back <a href="/wiki/Minority_governments_in_Canada" title="Minority governments in Canada">minority governments</a> during his tenure, and the Liberals not having a <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority</a> in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons" title="House of Commons">House of Commons</a> meant he needed support from the opposition parties. With that support, Pearson launched progressive policies such as <a href="/wiki/Medicare_(Canada)" title="Medicare (Canada)">universal health care</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Student_Loan_Program" class="mw-redirect" title="Canada Student Loan Program">Canada Student Loan Program</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Pension_Plan" title="Canada Pension Plan">Canada Pension Plan</a>. Pearson also introduced the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">Order of Canada</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission_on_Bilingualism_and_Biculturalism" title="Royal Commission on Bilingualism and Biculturalism">Royal Commission on Bilingualism and Biculturalism</a>, and oversaw <a href="/wiki/Great_Canadian_flag_debate" title="Great Canadian flag debate">the creation</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Canada" title="Flag of Canada">Maple Leaf flag</a> that was implemented in 1965. His government <a href="/wiki/Unification_of_the_Canadian_Armed_Forces" title="Unification of the Canadian Armed Forces">unified the Canadian Armed Forces</a> and kept <a href="/wiki/Canada_and_the_Vietnam_War" title="Canada and the Vietnam War">Canada out of the Vietnam War</a>. In 1967, Canada became the first country in the world to implement a <a href="/wiki/Points-based_immigration_system#Canada" title="Points-based immigration system">points-based immigration system</a>. After half a decade in power, Pearson resigned as prime minister and retired from politics.
</p><p>With his government programs and policies, together with his groundbreaking work at the United Nations and in international diplomacy, which included his role in ending the Suez Crisis, Pearson is generally considered among the most influential Canadians of the 20th century and is <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_Canada" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of Canada">ranked among the greatest Canadian prime ministers</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-irpp.org_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-irpp.org-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Maclean&#39;s_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Maclean&#39;s-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span id="Early_life.2C_family.2C_and_education"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life,_family,_and_education">Early life, family, and education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life, family, and education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Birthplace_of_Lester_B._Pearson_plaque_(photo_by_Djuradj_Vujcic).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Birthplace_of_Lester_B._Pearson_plaque_%28photo_by_Djuradj_Vujcic%29.jpg/195px-Birthplace_of_Lester_B._Pearson_plaque_%28photo_by_Djuradj_Vujcic%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="195" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Birthplace_of_Lester_B._Pearson_plaque_%28photo_by_Djuradj_Vujcic%29.jpg/293px-Birthplace_of_Lester_B._Pearson_plaque_%28photo_by_Djuradj_Vujcic%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Birthplace_of_Lester_B._Pearson_plaque_%28photo_by_Djuradj_Vujcic%29.jpg/390px-Birthplace_of_Lester_B._Pearson_plaque_%28photo_by_Djuradj_Vujcic%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="992" data-file-height="744" /></a><figcaption>A memorial plaque on the location of his birthplace</figcaption></figure>
<p>Pearson was born in <a href="/wiki/Newtonbrook" title="Newtonbrook">Newtonbrook</a> (now a part of <a href="/wiki/Toronto" title="Toronto">Toronto</a>) in the township of <a href="/wiki/York,_Toronto" class="mw-redirect" title="York, Toronto">York</a>, Ontario, the son of Annie Sarah (née Bowles) and Edwin Arthur Pearson, a <a href="/wiki/Methodism" title="Methodism">Methodist</a> (later <a href="/wiki/United_Church_of_Canada" title="United Church of Canada">United Church of Canada</a>) minister. Lester was the brother of Vaughan Whitier Pearson and Marmaduke (Duke) Pearson.<sup id="cite_ref-DictCdnBio_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DictCdnBio-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> When Pearson was one month old, his family moved to 1984 Yonge Street. Lester Pearson's father moved the young family north of Toronto to <a href="/wiki/Aurora,_Ontario" title="Aurora, Ontario">Aurora, Ontario</a>, where he was the minister at Aurora Methodist Church on <a href="/wiki/Yonge_Street" title="Yonge Street">Yonge Street</a>. Lester spent his early years in Aurora and attended the public school on Church Street. The family lived at 39 Catherine Avenue. Pearson was a member of the Aurora Rugby team.
</p><p>Pearson graduated from Hamilton Collegiate Institute in <a href="/wiki/Hamilton,_Ontario" title="Hamilton, Ontario">Hamilton, Ontario</a>, in 1913 at the age of 16. Later that same year, he entered <a href="/wiki/Victoria_University_in_the_University_of_Toronto" class="mw-redirect" title="Victoria University in the University of Toronto">Victoria College</a> at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Toronto" title="University of Toronto">University of Toronto</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-DictCdnBio_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DictCdnBio-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> where he lived in residence in Gate House and shared a room with his brother Duke. He was later elected to the <a href="/wiki/Pi_Gamma_Mu" title="Pi Gamma Mu">Pi Gamma Mu</a> <a href="/wiki/Social_science" title="Social science">social sciences</a> honour society's chapter at the University of Toronto for his outstanding scholastic performance in history and psychology. Just as <a href="/wiki/Norman_Jewison" title="Norman Jewison">Norman Jewison</a>, <a href="/wiki/E._J._Pratt" title="E. J. Pratt">E. J. Pratt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Northrop_Frye" title="Northrop Frye">Northrop Frye</a> and his student <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Atwood" title="Margaret Atwood">Margaret Atwood</a> would, Pearson participated in the sophomore theatrical tradition of <i>The Bob Comedy Revue</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> After Victoria College, Pearson won a scholarship to study at <a href="/wiki/St_John%27s_College,_Oxford" title="St John&#39;s College, Oxford">St John's College, Oxford</a>, from 1921 to 1923.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sporting_interests">Sporting interests</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Sporting interests">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>At the University of Toronto, Pearson became a noted athlete, excelling in <a href="/wiki/Rugby_union" title="Rugby union">rugby union</a> and also playing <a href="/wiki/Basketball" title="Basketball">basketball</a>. He later also played for the <a href="/wiki/Oxford_University_Ice_Hockey_Club" title="Oxford University Ice Hockey Club">Oxford University Ice Hockey Club</a> while on a scholarship at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Oxford" title="University of Oxford">University of Oxford</a>, a team that won the first <a href="/wiki/Spengler_Cup" title="Spengler Cup">Spengler Cup</a> in 1923. Pearson also excelled in <a href="/wiki/Baseball" title="Baseball">baseball</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lacrosse" title="Lacrosse">lacrosse</a> as a youth. His baseball talents as an <a href="/wiki/Infielder" title="Infielder">infielder</a> were strong enough for a summer of semi-pro play with the <a href="/wiki/Guelph_Maple_Leafs" class="mw-redirect" title="Guelph Maple Leafs">Guelph Maple Leafs</a> of the Ontario <a href="/wiki/Intercounty_Baseball_League" title="Intercounty Baseball League">Intercounty Baseball League</a>. Pearson toured North America with a combined Oxford and Cambridge Universities lacrosse team in 1923. After he joined the University of Toronto History Department as an instructor, he helped to coach the U of T's football and hockey teams. He played <a href="/wiki/Golf" title="Golf">golf</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tennis" title="Tennis">tennis</a> to high standards as an adult.<sup id="cite_ref-LifeofLP_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LifeofLP-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="First_World_War">First World War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: First World War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pearson_in_World_War_One.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Pearson_in_World_War_One.jpg/150px-Pearson_in_World_War_One.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="193" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Pearson_in_World_War_One.jpg/225px-Pearson_in_World_War_One.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Pearson_in_World_War_One.jpg/300px-Pearson_in_World_War_One.jpg 2x" data-file-width="374" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>Pearson serving with the Canadian Army Medical Corps in World War I in <a href="/wiki/Thessaloniki" title="Thessaloniki">Salonika</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>During <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, Pearson volunteered for service as a medical orderly with the University of Toronto Hospital Unit. In 1915, he entered overseas service with the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Army_Medical_Corps" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Army Medical Corps">Canadian Army Medical Corps</a> as a stretcher-bearer with the rank of <a href="/wiki/Private_(rank)" title="Private (rank)">private</a>, and was subsequently promoted to <a href="/wiki/Corporal" title="Corporal">corporal</a>. During this period of service, he spent nearly two years in Southern Europe, being shipped to <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a> and thereafter served on the <a href="/wiki/Salonika_front" class="mw-redirect" title="Salonika front">Salonika front</a>. He also served alongside the <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Campaign_of_World_War_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Serbian Campaign of World War I">Serbian Army</a> as a medical orderly.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Oldest_Filling_in_the_World_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Oldest_Filling_in_the_World-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> On 2 August 1917, Pearson was commissioned a temporary lieutenant.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Air_Force" title="Royal Canadian Air Force">Royal Canadian Air Force</a> did not exist at that time, so Pearson transferred to Britain's <a href="/wiki/Royal_Flying_Corps" title="Royal Flying Corps">Royal Flying Corps</a>, where he served as a <a href="/wiki/Flying_officer" title="Flying officer">flying officer</a>. It was as a pilot that he received the nickname of "Mike", given to him by a flight instructor who felt that "Lester" was too mild a name for an airman: "That’s a sissy’s name. You’re Mike," the instructor said.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Thereafter, Pearson would use the name "Lester" on official documents and in public life, but was always addressed as "Mike" by friends and family.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pearson learned to fly at an air training school in <a href="/wiki/Hendon" title="Hendon">Hendon</a>, England. He survived an airplane crash during his first flight.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> In 1918, Pearson was hit by a bus in London during a citywide <a href="/wiki/Blackout_(wartime)" title="Blackout (wartime)">blackout</a> and he was sent home to recuperate, but then he was discharged from the service.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Inter-war_years">Inter-war years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Inter-war years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ice_hockey_1922.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Ice_hockey_1922.jpg/220px-Ice_hockey_1922.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="126" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Ice_hockey_1922.jpg/330px-Ice_hockey_1922.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Ice_hockey_1922.jpg/440px-Ice_hockey_1922.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="366" /></a><figcaption>Ice hockey in Europe; Oxford University vs. <a href="/wiki/Switzerland" title="Switzerland">Switzerland</a>, 1922. Future Canadian Prime Minister Lester Pearson is at right front. His nickname from the Swiss was "Herr Zig-Zag".</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the war, he returned to school, receiving his <a href="/wiki/Bachelor_of_Arts_degree" class="mw-redirect" title="Bachelor of Arts degree">Bachelor of Arts degree</a> from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Toronto" title="University of Toronto">University of Toronto</a> in 1919.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> He was able to complete his degree after one more term, under a ruling in force at the time, since he had served in the military during the war. He and his brother Duke then spent a year working in <a href="/wiki/Hamilton,_Ontario" title="Hamilton, Ontario">Hamilton, Ontario</a>, and in Chicago, in the meat-packing industry at <a href="/wiki/Armour_and_Company" title="Armour and Company">Armour and Company</a> (who's president at the Frank Edson White was his uncle through marriage to aunt Lillian Sophia Pearson White<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>),<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> which he did not enjoy.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Oxford">Oxford</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Oxford">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Upon receiving a scholarship from the <a href="/wiki/Massey_Foundation" title="Massey Foundation">Massey Foundation</a>, he studied for two years at <a href="/wiki/St_John%27s_College,_Oxford" title="St John&#39;s College, Oxford">St John's College</a> at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Oxford" title="University of Oxford">University of Oxford</a>, where he received a B.A. degree with Second-Class honours in modern history in 1923, and the <a href="/wiki/Degrees_of_Oxford_University" class="mw-redirect" title="Degrees of Oxford University">M.A.</a> in 1925. After Oxford, he returned to Canada and taught history at the University of Toronto.
</p>
<h3><span id="Marriage.2C_family"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Marriage,_family">Marriage, family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Marriage, family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Ross_Mclean.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/John_Ross_Mclean.jpg/170px-John_Ross_Mclean.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="234" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/John_Ross_Mclean.jpg/255px-John_Ross_Mclean.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/John_Ross_Mclean.jpg/340px-John_Ross_Mclean.jpg 2x" data-file-width="780" data-file-height="1074" /></a><figcaption>Pearson with <a href="/wiki/Ross_McLean_(civil_servant)" title="Ross McLean (civil servant)">John Ross McLean</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vincent_Massey" title="Vincent Massey">Vincent Massey</a> and <a href="/wiki/Georges_Vanier" title="Georges Vanier">Georges Vanier</a> on 1 January 1938 at <a href="/wiki/Canada_House" title="Canada House">Canada House</a>, London</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1925, he married <a href="/wiki/Maryon_Moody" class="mw-redirect" title="Maryon Moody">Maryon Moody</a>, from Winnipeg, who had been one of his students at the University of Toronto. Together, they had one son, <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Pearson" title="Geoffrey Pearson">Geoffrey</a>, and one daughter, Patricia.<sup id="cite_ref-LifeofLP_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LifeofLP-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Maryon was confident and outspoken and she supported her husband in all his political endeavours.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Diplomat.2C_public_servant"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Diplomat,_public_servant">Diplomat, public servant</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Diplomat, public servant">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1927, after scoring the top marks on the Canadian foreign service entry exam, he then embarked on a career in the <a href="/wiki/Foreign_Affairs_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Foreign Affairs Canada">Department of External Affairs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-LifeofLP_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LifeofLP-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/R._B._Bennett" title="R. B. Bennett">R. B. Bennett</a> was a noted talent spotter. He took note of, and encouraged, the young Lester Pearson in the early 1930s, and appointed Pearson to significant roles on two major government inquiries: the 1931 Royal Commission on Grain Futures, and the 1934 Royal Commission on Price Spreads. Bennett saw that Pearson was recognized with an <a href="/wiki/OBE" class="mw-redirect" title="OBE">OBE</a> after he shone in that work, arranged a bonus of $1,800, and invited him to a London conference. Pearson was assigned to the <a href="/wiki/High_Commission_of_Canada_to_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="High Commission of Canada to the United Kingdom">High Commission of Canada to the United Kingdom</a> in 1935.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="World_War_II_and_aftermath">World War II and aftermath</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: World War II and aftermath">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lester_Bowles_Pearson_presiding_at_a_plenary_session_of_the_founding_conference_of_the_United_Nations_Food_and_Agriculture_Organization.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Lester_Bowles_Pearson_presiding_at_a_plenary_session_of_the_founding_conference_of_the_United_Nations_Food_and_Agriculture_Organization.jpg/220px-Lester_Bowles_Pearson_presiding_at_a_plenary_session_of_the_founding_conference_of_the_United_Nations_Food_and_Agriculture_Organization.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="172" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Lester_Bowles_Pearson_presiding_at_a_plenary_session_of_the_founding_conference_of_the_United_Nations_Food_and_Agriculture_Organization.jpg/330px-Lester_Bowles_Pearson_presiding_at_a_plenary_session_of_the_founding_conference_of_the_United_Nations_Food_and_Agriculture_Organization.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Lester_Bowles_Pearson_presiding_at_a_plenary_session_of_the_founding_conference_of_the_United_Nations_Food_and_Agriculture_Organization.jpg/440px-Lester_Bowles_Pearson_presiding_at_a_plenary_session_of_the_founding_conference_of_the_United_Nations_Food_and_Agriculture_Organization.jpg 2x" data-file-width="610" data-file-height="477" /></a><figcaption>Pearson presiding at a plenary session of the founding conference of the United Nations <a href="/wiki/Food_and_Agriculture_Organization" title="Food and Agriculture Organization">Food and Agriculture Organization</a> in 1945.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Pearson continued to serve at <a href="/wiki/Canada_House" title="Canada House">Canada House</a> during <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a> from 1939 through 1942 as the second-in-command, where he coordinated military supply and refugee problems, serving under High Commissioner <a href="/wiki/Vincent_Massey" title="Vincent Massey">Vincent Massey</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-LifeofLP_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LifeofLP-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pearson returned to <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a> for a few months, where he was an assistant under secretary from 1941 through 1942.<sup id="cite_ref-EncyclopediaCanadiana_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EncyclopediaCanadiana-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> In June 1942 he was posted to the Canadian Embassy in Washington, D.C., as a ministerial counsellor.<sup id="cite_ref-EncyclopediaCanadiana_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EncyclopediaCanadiana-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> He served as second-in-command for nearly two years. Promoted minister <a href="/wiki/Plenipotentiary" title="Plenipotentiary">plenipotentiary</a> in 1944, he became the second <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Ambassador_to_the_United_States" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Ambassador to the United States">Canadian Ambassador to the United States</a> on 1 January 1945. He remained in this position through September 1946.<sup id="cite_ref-LifeofLP_5-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LifeofLP-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-EncyclopediaCanadiana_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EncyclopediaCanadiana-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pearson had an important part in founding both the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a> and the <a href="/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty_Organization" class="mw-redirect" title="North Atlantic Treaty Organization">North Atlantic Treaty Organization</a> (NATO).<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pearson nearly became the first <a href="/wiki/Secretary-General_of_the_United_Nations" title="Secretary-General of the United Nations">Secretary-General of the United Nations</a> in 1946, but he was vetoed by the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-LifeofLP_5-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LifeofLP-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> He was also the leading candidate for Secretary-General in the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Secretary-General_selection,_1953" class="mw-redirect" title="United Nations Secretary-General selection, 1953">1953 selection</a>, when the British conducted a vigorous campaign on his behalf. He placed first with 10 out of 11 votes in the Security Council, but the lone negative vote was another Soviet veto.<sup id="cite_ref-nytimes19530313_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes19530313-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-scr_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-scr-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Security Council instead settled on <a href="/wiki/Dag_Hammarskj%C3%B6ld" title="Dag Hammarskjöld">Dag Hammarskjöld</a> of Sweden; all UN Secretaries-General would come from neutral countries for the rest of the Cold War.
</p><p>The Canadian Prime Minister, <a href="/wiki/Mackenzie_King" class="mw-redirect" title="Mackenzie King">Mackenzie King</a>, tried to recruit Pearson into his government as the war wound down. Pearson felt honoured by King's approach, but he resisted at the time, due to his personal dislike of King's poor personal style and political methods.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Pearson did not make the move into politics until a few years later, after King had announced his retirement as the Prime Minister of Canada.
</p>
<h2><span id="Secretary_of_State_for_External_Affairs_.281948-1957.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Secretary_of_State_for_External_Affairs_(1948-1957)">Secretary of State for External Affairs (1948-1957)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Secretary of State for External Affairs (1948-1957)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg/250px-StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="196" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg/375px-StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg/500px-StLaurnet_Pearson_and_Churchill.jpg 2x" data-file-width="760" data-file-height="597" /></a><figcaption>Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Louis_St._Laurent" title="Louis St. Laurent">Louis St. Laurent</a> (far left) and Pearson (far right) welcome <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">UK Prime Minister</a> Sir <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> and Foreign Secretary Sir <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Eden" title="Anthony Eden">Anthony Eden</a> at <a href="/wiki/Ottawa/Rockcliffe_Airport" title="Ottawa/Rockcliffe Airport">Rockcliffe Airport</a>, Ottawa, on 29 June 1954.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:LevesqueinterviewsPearsoninMoscow.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/LevesqueinterviewsPearsoninMoscow.jpg/220px-LevesqueinterviewsPearsoninMoscow.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/LevesqueinterviewsPearsoninMoscow.jpg/330px-LevesqueinterviewsPearsoninMoscow.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/LevesqueinterviewsPearsoninMoscow.jpg/440px-LevesqueinterviewsPearsoninMoscow.jpg 2x" data-file-width="555" data-file-height="392" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Levesque" class="mw-redirect" title="René Levesque">René Levesque</a> interviews Pearson in Moscow, 1955</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1948, before his retirement, Prime Minister King appointed Pearson <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_External_Affairs_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for External Affairs (Canada)">Secretary of State for External Affairs</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a> government. Shortly afterward, Pearson won a seat in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons</a>, for the federal riding of <a href="/wiki/Algoma_East" title="Algoma East">Algoma East</a> in <a href="/wiki/Northern_Ontario" title="Northern Ontario">Northern Ontario</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Pearson then served as Secretary of State for External Affairs for Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Louis_St._Laurent" title="Louis St. Laurent">Louis St. Laurent</a>, until the defeat of the St. Laurent government in 1957.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Role_in_Suez_crisis_leads_to_Nobel_Peace_Prize">Role in Suez crisis leads to Nobel Peace Prize</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Role in Suez crisis leads to Nobel Peace Prize">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1957, for his role in resolving the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Crisis" title="Suez Crisis">Suez Crisis</a> through the United Nations one year earlier, Pearson was awarded the <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> The selection committee argued that Pearson had "saved the world", but critics accused him of betraying the motherland and Canada's ties with the UK. Pearson and UN Secretary-General <a href="/wiki/Dag_Hammarskj%C3%B6ld" title="Dag Hammarskjöld">Dag Hammarskjöld</a> are considered the fathers of the modern concept of <a href="/wiki/Peacekeeping" title="Peacekeeping">peacekeeping</a>. Together, they were able to organize the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Emergency_Force" title="United Nations Emergency Force">United Nations Emergency Force</a> by way of a five-day fly-around in early November 1956 after the <a href="/wiki/First_emergency_special_session_of_the_United_Nations_General_Assembly" title="First emergency special session of the United Nations General Assembly">First emergency special session of the United Nations General Assembly</a>. His Nobel medal was on permanent display in the front lobby of the Lester B. Pearson Building, the headquarters of <a href="/wiki/Global_Affairs_Canada" title="Global Affairs Canada">Global Affairs Canada</a> in Ottawa until 2017 when the medal was loaned to the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Museum_of_History" title="Canadian Museum of History">Canadian Museum of History</a>, to be displayed in the 'Canadian History Hall'.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Party_leadership">Party leadership</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Party leadership">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lester_Pearson_at_election_campaign_event_(50540638661).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Lester_Pearson_at_election_campaign_event_%2850540638661%29.jpg/220px-Lester_Pearson_at_election_campaign_event_%2850540638661%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="172" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Lester_Pearson_at_election_campaign_event_%2850540638661%29.jpg/330px-Lester_Pearson_at_election_campaign_event_%2850540638661%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Lester_Pearson_at_election_campaign_event_%2850540638661%29.jpg/440px-Lester_Pearson_at_election_campaign_event_%2850540638661%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="942" data-file-height="738" /></a><figcaption>Pearson campaigning for <a href="/wiki/Bruce_Beer" title="Bruce Beer">Bruce Beer</a> in <a href="/wiki/Peel_(federal_electoral_district)" title="Peel (federal electoral district)">Peel</a> during the 1962 Federal election</figcaption></figure>
<p>St. Laurent was defeated by the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservatives</a> under <a href="/wiki/John_Diefenbaker" title="John Diefenbaker">John Diefenbaker</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1957_Canadian_federal_election" title="1957 Canadian federal election">election of 1957</a>. After just a few months as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Leader of the Opposition (Canada)">Leader of the Opposition</a>, St. Laurent retired, and he endorsed Pearson as his successor. Pearson was elected leader of the Liberal Party at its <a href="/wiki/1958_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1958 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">leadership convention of 1958</a>, defeating his chief rival, former cabinet minister <a href="/wiki/Paul_Martin_Sr." title="Paul Martin Sr.">Paul Martin Sr.</a>
</p><p>At his first parliamentary session as opposition leader, Pearson asked Diefenbaker to give power back to the Liberals without an election, because of a recent economic downturn. This strategy backfired when Diefenbaker showed a classified Liberal document saying that the economy would face a downturn in that year. This contrasted heavily with the Liberals' campaign promises of 1957.
</p><p>Consequently, Pearson's party was routed in the <a href="/wiki/1958_Canadian_federal_election" title="1958 Canadian federal election">federal election of 1958</a>. The Liberals lost more than half their seats, while Diefenbaker's Conservatives won the largest majority ever seen in Canada to that point (208 of 265 seats). Furthermore, the election cost the Liberals their stronghold in <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>. This province had voted largely Liberal in federal elections since the <a href="/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1917" title="Conscription Crisis of 1917">Conscription Crisis of 1917</a>, but Quebec had no <a href="/wiki/Favourite_son" class="mw-redirect" title="Favourite son">favourite son</a> leader, as it had had since 1948.
</p><p>Pearson convened a significant "Thinkers' Conference" at <a href="/wiki/Kingston,_Ontario" title="Kingston, Ontario">Kingston, Ontario</a> in 1960. This event developed many of the ideas later implemented when he became the Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/1962_Canadian_federal_election" title="1962 Canadian federal election">federal election of 1962</a>, the Liberals, led by Pearson, and the surprise election of 30 <a href="/wiki/Social_Credit" class="mw-redirect" title="Social Credit">Social Credit</a> MP's, deprived the Tories of their majority. As a consequence, Diefenbaker now had to preside over a <a href="/wiki/Minority_governments_in_Canada" title="Minority governments in Canada">minority government</a>.
</p><p>Not long after the election, Pearson capitalized on the Conservatives' indecision on accepting American <a href="/wiki/Nuclear_warhead" class="mw-redirect" title="Nuclear warhead">nuclear warheads</a> on Canadian <a href="/wiki/BOMARC_missile" class="mw-redirect" title="BOMARC missile">BOMARC missiles</a>. Defence Minister <a href="/wiki/Douglas_Harkness" title="Douglas Harkness">Douglas Harkness</a> resigned from Cabinet on 4 February 1963, because of Diefenbaker's opposition to accepting the warheads. On the next day, the government lost two nonconfidence motions on the issue, <a href="/wiki/1963_Canadian_federal_election" title="1963 Canadian federal election">forcing a national election</a>. In that election, the Liberals took 129 seats to the Tories' 95. Despite winning 41 percent of the vote, the Liberals came up five seats short of a majority largely because of winning just three seats on the Prairies. With the support of six Social Credit MPs from Quebec,<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Pearson was able to guarantee stable government to the Governor-General, and Diefenbaker resigned, allowing Pearson to form a <a href="/wiki/Minority_governments_in_Canada" title="Minority governments in Canada">minority government</a>. He was sworn in as prime minister on 22 April 1963.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_.281963.E2.80.931968.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_(1963–1968)">Prime Minister (1963–1968)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Prime Minister (1963–1968)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_policy_and_events">Domestic policy and events</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Domestic policy and events">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Pearson campaigned during the 1963 election promising "60 Days of Decision" and supported the <a href="/wiki/Bomarc" class="mw-redirect" title="Bomarc">Bomarc</a> <a href="/wiki/Surface-to-air_missile" title="Surface-to-air missile">surface-to-air missile</a> program. Pearson never had a majority in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons</a>, but he brought in many of Canada's major updated social programs, including <a href="/wiki/Medicare_(Canada)" title="Medicare (Canada)">universal health care</a> (though that credit should be shared with <a href="/wiki/Tommy_Douglas" title="Tommy Douglas">Tommy Douglas</a>, who as premier of Saskatchewan had introduced the country's first medicare system), the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Pension_Plan" title="Canada Pension Plan">Canada Pension Plan</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Canada_Student_Loans" class="mw-redirect" title="Canada Student Loans">Canada Student Loans</a>. Pearson instituted a new national flag, the <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Canada" title="Flag of Canada">Maple Leaf flag</a>, after a national debate known as the <a href="/wiki/Great_Canadian_flag_debate" title="Great Canadian flag debate">Great Canadian flag debate</a>. He also instituted the 40-hour work week, two weeks vacation time, and a new <a href="/wiki/Minimum_wage" title="Minimum wage">minimum wage</a> for workers in federally-regulated areas.
</p><p>Pearson also started a number of <a href="/wiki/Royal_commission" title="Royal commission">royal commissions</a>, including the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission_on_the_Status_of_Women" title="Royal Commission on the Status of Women">Royal Commission on the Status of Women</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission_on_Bilingualism_and_Biculturalism" title="Royal Commission on Bilingualism and Biculturalism">Royal Commission on Bilingualism and Biculturalism</a>. These suggested changes that helped create legal equality for women and brought official <a href="/wiki/Bilingualism_in_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Bilingualism in Canada">bilingualism</a> into being. After Pearson's term in office, French was made an <a href="/wiki/Official_language" title="Official language">official language</a>, and the Canadian government provided services in both English and French. Pearson himself had hoped that he would be the last unilingual Prime Minister of Canada and fluency in both English and French became an unofficial requirement for candidates for Prime Minister after Pearson left office.
</p><p>In 1967, Pearson's government introduced a discrimination-free <a href="/wiki/Points-based_immigration_system" title="Points-based immigration system">points-based system</a> which encouraged immigration to Canada, making it the first country in the world to do so.
</p><p>Pearson oversaw <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Centennial" title="Canadian Centennial">Canada's centennial celebrations</a> in 1967 before retiring. The Canadian news agency, <a href="/wiki/The_Canadian_Press" title="The Canadian Press">The Canadian Press</a>, named him "<a href="/wiki/Canadian_Newsmaker_of_the_Year" title="Canadian Newsmaker of the Year">Newsmaker of the Year</a>" that year, citing his leadership during the centennial celebrations, which brought the <a href="/wiki/Centennial_Flame" title="Centennial Flame">Centennial Flame</a> to <a href="/wiki/Parliament_Hill" title="Parliament Hill">Parliament Hill</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Foreign policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Trudeau,_Turner,_Chretien,_and_Pearson.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/1a/Trudeau%2C_Turner%2C_Chretien%2C_and_Pearson.jpg/220px-Trudeau%2C_Turner%2C_Chretien%2C_and_Pearson.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="145" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/1a/Trudeau%2C_Turner%2C_Chretien%2C_and_Pearson.jpg/330px-Trudeau%2C_Turner%2C_Chretien%2C_and_Pearson.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/1/1a/Trudeau%2C_Turner%2C_Chretien%2C_and_Pearson.jpg 2x" data-file-width="389" data-file-height="256" /></a><figcaption>Pearson, and three of his cabinet ministers who later became Prime Ministers. From left to right, <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Turner" title="John Turner">John Turner</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a>, and Pearson.</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 15 January 1964, Pearson became the first Canadian Prime Minister to make an official <a href="/wiki/State_visit" title="State visit">state visit</a> to France.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1967, French president <a href="/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle" title="Charles de Gaulle">Charles de Gaulle</a> made a visit to <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>. A staunch advocate of Quebec separatism, de Gaulle went so far as to say that his procession in <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a> reminded him of his return to Paris after it was freed from the <a href="/wiki/Nazism" title="Nazism">Nazis</a> during the Second World War. President de Gaulle also gave his <a href="/wiki/Vive_le_Qu%C3%A9bec_libre_speech" class="mw-redirect" title="Vive le Québec libre speech">"Vive le Québec libre" speech</a> during the visit. Given Canada's efforts in aiding France during both world wars, Pearson was enraged. He rebuked de Gaulle in a speech the following day, remarking that "Canadians do not need to be liberated", and made it clear that de Gaulle was no longer welcome in Canada.
</p><p>Pearson signed the <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_Automotive_Products_Agreement" title="Canada–United States Automotive Products Agreement">Canada–United States Automotive Agreement</a> (or Auto Pact) in January 1965, and unemployment fell to its lowest rate in over a decade.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While in office, Pearson declined U.S. requests to send Canadian combat troops into the <a href="/wiki/Vietnam_War" title="Vietnam War">Vietnam War</a>. Pearson spoke at <a href="/wiki/Temple_University" title="Temple University">Temple University</a> in <a href="/wiki/Philadelphia" title="Philadelphia">Philadelphia</a> on 2 April 1965 and voiced his support for a pause in the American bombing of North Vietnam, so that a diplomatic solution to the crisis might unfold. To President <a href="/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson" title="Lyndon B. Johnson">Lyndon B. Johnson</a>, this criticism of American foreign policy on American soil was intolerable. Before Pearson had finished his speech, he was invited to <a href="/wiki/Camp_David,_Maryland" class="mw-redirect" title="Camp David, Maryland">Camp David, Maryland</a>, to meet with Johnson the next day. Johnson, who was notorious for his personal touch in politics, reportedly grabbed Pearson by the lapels and shouted, "You pissed on my rug!"<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Text of his Philadelphia speech, however, showed that Pearson in fact supported President Johnson's policy in Vietnam, even stating "The government and great majority of people of my country have supported wholeheartedly the US peacekeeping and peacemaking policies in Vietnam."<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After this incident, Johnson and Pearson did have further contacts, including two more meetings together, both times in Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Canada's exported raw materials and resources helped fuel and sustain American efforts in the Vietnam War.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military">Military</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Military">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Pearson's government endured significant controversy in Canada's military services throughout the mid-1960s, following the tabling of the <a href="/wiki/White_Paper_on_Defence" class="mw-redirect" title="White Paper on Defence">White Paper on Defence</a> in March 1964. This document laid out a plan to merge the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Navy" title="Royal Canadian Navy">Royal Canadian Navy</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Air_Force" title="Royal Canadian Air Force">Royal Canadian Air Force</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Army" title="Canadian Army">Canadian Army</a> to form a single service called the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Forces" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Forces">Canadian Forces</a>. Military unification took effect on 1 February 1968, when <i>The Canadian Forces Reorganization Act</i> received Royal Assent.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Supreme_Court_appointments">Supreme Court appointments</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Supreme Court appointments">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lester_Pearson_statue_Ottawa.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Lester_Pearson_statue_Ottawa.jpg/220px-Lester_Pearson_statue_Ottawa.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="321" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Lester_Pearson_statue_Ottawa.jpg/330px-Lester_Pearson_statue_Ottawa.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Lester_Pearson_statue_Ottawa.jpg/440px-Lester_Pearson_statue_Ottawa.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2088" data-file-height="3042" /></a><figcaption>Statue on Parliament Hill grounds</figcaption></figure>
<p>Pearson chose the following jurists to be appointed as justices of the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court of Canada</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">Governor General</a>:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Robert_Taschereau" title="Robert Taschereau">Robert Taschereau</a> (as chief justice, 22 April 1963 – 1 September 1967; appointed a <a href="/wiki/Puisne_justice" class="mw-redirect" title="Puisne justice">puisne justice</a> under Prime Minister King, 9 February 1940)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Wishart_Flett_Spence" class="mw-redirect" title="Wishart Flett Spence">Wishart Flett Spence</a> (30 May 1963 – 29 December 1978)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/John_Robert_Cartwright" title="John Robert Cartwright">John Robert Cartwright</a> (as Chief Justice, 1 September 1967 – 23 March 1970; appointed a Puisne Justice under Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Louis_St._Laurent" title="Louis St. Laurent">St. Laurent</a>, 22 December 1949)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Louis-Philippe_Pigeon" title="Louis-Philippe Pigeon">Louis-Philippe Pigeon</a> (21 September 1967 – 8 February 1980)</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement">Retirement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Retirement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After his 14 December 1967 announcement that he was retiring from politics, <a href="/wiki/1968_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1968 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">a leadership convention</a> was held. Pearson's successor was <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a>, whom Pearson had recruited and made <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Justice (Canada)">justice minister</a> in his <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Canada" title="Cabinet of Canada">cabinet</a>. Two other cabinet ministers Pearson had recruited, <a href="/wiki/John_Turner" title="John Turner">John Turner</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a>, served as prime ministers following Trudeau's retirement.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="After_politics">After politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: After politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>From 1968 to 1969, Pearson served as chairman of the Commission on International Development (<a href="/wiki/Pearson_Commission_on_International_Development" title="Pearson Commission on International Development">Pearson Commission on International Development</a>), which was sponsored by the <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a>. Following his retirement, he lectured at <a href="/wiki/Carleton_University" title="Carleton University">Carleton University</a> in Ottawa while writing his memoirs. From 1970 to 1972, he was the first chairman of the Board of Governors of the <a href="/wiki/International_Development_Research_Centre" title="International Development Research Centre">International Development Research Centre</a>. From 1969 until his death in 1972, he was chancellor of Carleton University.
</p><p>Pearson had planned to write a three-volume set of memoirs with the title "<i>Mike: The Memoirs of the Rt. Hon. Lester B. Pearson</i>". The first volume was published in 1972. The other two volumes, after his death, were published in 1973 and 1975 but they lack the authenticity due to apparent bias from the <a href="/wiki/Ghostwriters" class="mw-redirect" title="Ghostwriters">ghostwriters</a> who wrote them.<sup id="cite_ref-extraordinary_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-extraordinary-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-SYMPOSIUM_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SYMPOSIUM-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Illness_and_death">Illness and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Illness and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lester_B_Pearson_Gravestone_WakefieldQC.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Lester_B_Pearson_Gravestone_WakefieldQC.JPG/220px-Lester_B_Pearson_Gravestone_WakefieldQC.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Lester_B_Pearson_Gravestone_WakefieldQC.JPG/330px-Lester_B_Pearson_Gravestone_WakefieldQC.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Lester_B_Pearson_Gravestone_WakefieldQC.JPG/440px-Lester_B_Pearson_Gravestone_WakefieldQC.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2272" data-file-height="1704" /></a><figcaption>Pearson's gravestone in <a href="/wiki/Wakefield,_Quebec" title="Wakefield, Quebec">Wakefield, Quebec</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1970, Pearson underwent a surgery to have his right eye removed to remove a tumor in that area.<sup id="cite_ref-tumour_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tumour-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 1972, it was reported that he was admitted to the hospital for further unspecified treatment, but the prognosis was poor. He tried to write at this juncture the story of his prime ministerial career, but his condition, which was already precarious, deteriorated rapidly by Christmas Eve.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 27 December 1972, it was announced that the cancer had spread to the liver and Pearson had lapsed into a coma. He died at 11:40&#160;pm <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Time_Zone" title="Eastern Time Zone">ET</a> on 27 December 1972 in his Ottawa home.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pearson is buried at <a href="/wiki/Maclaren_Cemetery" title="Maclaren Cemetery">Maclaren Cemetery</a> in <a href="/wiki/Wakefield,_Quebec" title="Wakefield, Quebec">Wakefield, Quebec</a><sup id="cite_ref-Parks_Grave_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Parks_Grave-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> (just north of <a href="/wiki/Gatineau,_Quebec" class="mw-redirect" title="Gatineau, Quebec">Gatineau</a>), next to his close External Affairs colleagues <a href="/wiki/H._H._Wrong" class="mw-redirect" title="H. H. Wrong">H. H. Wrong</a> and <a href="/wiki/Norman_Robertson" title="Norman Robertson">Norman Robertson</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours_and_awards">Honours and awards</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Honours and awards">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<td><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:World_War_I_Victory_Medal_ribbon.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/World_War_I_Victory_Medal_ribbon.svg/80px-World_War_I_Victory_Medal_ribbon.svg.png" decoding="async" width="80" height="22" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/World_War_I_Victory_Medal_ribbon.svg/120px-World_War_I_Victory_Medal_ribbon.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/World_War_I_Victory_Medal_ribbon.svg/160px-World_War_I_Victory_Medal_ribbon.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="279" /></a></span></td>
<td><a href="/wiki/Victory_Medal_(United_Kingdom)" title="Victory Medal (United Kingdom)">Victory Medal (United Kingdom)</a></td>
<td>
<ul><li>As a member of the Canadian Armed Forces</li></ul>
</td></tr>
<tr>
<td><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Ribbon_-_QE_II_Coronation_Medal.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Ribbon_-_QE_II_Coronation_Medal.png/82px-Ribbon_-_QE_II_Coronation_Medal.png" decoding="async" width="82" height="25" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Ribbon_-_QE_II_Coronation_Medal.png/125px-Ribbon_-_QE_II_Coronation_Medal.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Ribbon_-_QE_II_Coronation_Medal.png/164px-Ribbon_-_QE_II_Coronation_Medal.png 2x" data-file-width="320" data-file-height="98" /></a></span></td>
<td><a href="/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_II_Coronation_Medal" title="Queen Elizabeth II Coronation Medal">Queen Elizabeth II Coronation Medal</a></td>
<td>
<ul><li>1953</li>
<li>As a member of the Queen's Privy Council for Canada and an elected Member of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons of Canada</a>, the then Honourable Lester B. Pearson, P.C., O.B.E., M.P., would be awarded the medal as a member of the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_order_of_precedence" title="Canadian order of precedence">Canadian order of precedence</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-dominionofcanada.com_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dominionofcanada.com-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
</td></tr>
<tr>
<td><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Canada100_ribbon.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Canada100_ribbon.png/80px-Canada100_ribbon.png" decoding="async" width="80" height="24" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b0/Canada100_ribbon.png 1.5x" data-file-width="100" data-file-height="30" /></a></span></td>
<td><a href="/wiki/Canadian_Centennial_Medal" title="Canadian Centennial Medal">Centennial Anniversary of the Confederation of Canada Medal</a></td>
<td>
<ul><li>1967</li>
<li>As the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Canada" title="Prime Minister of Canada">Prime Minister of Canada</a> and an elected Member of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons of Canada</a>, the Right Honourable Lester B. Pearson would be awarded the medal as a member of the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_order_of_precedence" title="Canadian order of precedence">Canadian order of precedence</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-dominionofcanada.com_47-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dominionofcanada.com-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
</td></tr>
</tbody></table>
<ul><li>Elected a Foreign Honorary Member of the <a href="/wiki/American_Academy_of_Arts_and_Sciences" title="American Academy of Arts and Sciences">American Academy of Arts and Sciences</a> in 1957.<sup id="cite_ref-AAAS_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AAAS-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Press" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Press">Canadian Press</a> named Pearson "<a href="/wiki/Canadian_Newsmaker_of_the_Year" title="Canadian Newsmaker of the Year">Newsmaker of the Year</a>" nine times, a record he held until his successor, Pierre Trudeau, surpassed it in 2000. He was also only one of two prime ministers to have received the honour both before and when prime minister (the other being Brian Mulroney).</li>
<li>Pearson was inducted into the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Canadian_Peace_Hall_of_Fame&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Canadian Peace Hall of Fame (page does not exist)">Canadian Peace Hall of Fame</a> in 2000.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Pearson_Medal_of_Peace" title="Pearson Medal of Peace">Pearson Medal of Peace</a>, first awarded in 1979, is an award given out annually by the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Association_in_Canada" title="United Nations Association in Canada">United Nations Association in Canada</a> to recognize an individual Canadian's "contribution to international service".</li>
<li>A plaque, placed by the <a href="/wiki/Ontario_Heritage_Trust" title="Ontario Heritage Trust">Ontario Heritage Trust</a>, is on the grounds of Newtonbrook United Church, the successor congregation to the one that owned the manse.<sup id="cite_ref-TorontoPlaques_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TorontoPlaques-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In a survey by Canadian historians of the first 20 Prime Ministers through <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a>, Pearson ranked No.&#160;6.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In a survey by Canadian historians of the Canadian prime ministers who served after World War II, Pearson was ranked first "by a landslide".<sup id="cite_ref-irpp.org_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-irpp.org-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Order_of_Canada_Citation">Order of Canada Citation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Order of Canada Citation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Pearson was appointed a Companion of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">Order of Canada</a> on 28 June 1968. His citation reads:<sup id="cite_ref-Governor_General_of_Canada_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Governor_General_of_Canada-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>Former Prime Minister of Canada. For his services to Canada at home and abroad.</p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Educational_and_academic_institutions">Educational and academic institutions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Educational and academic institutions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:LPB_quote_on_Peacekeeping_Monument.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/LPB_quote_on_Peacekeeping_Monument.jpg/220px-LPB_quote_on_Peacekeeping_Monument.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="95" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/LPB_quote_on_Peacekeeping_Monument.jpg/330px-LPB_quote_on_Peacekeeping_Monument.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/LPB_quote_on_Peacekeeping_Monument.jpg/440px-LPB_quote_on_Peacekeeping_Monument.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1780" data-file-height="768" /></a><figcaption>Lester B. Pearson quote on the <a href="/wiki/Peacekeeping_Monument" title="Peacekeeping Monument">Peacekeeping Monument</a></figcaption></figure>
<ul><li>The <a href="/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson_United_World_College" class="mw-redirect" title="Lester B. Pearson United World College">Lester B. Pearson United World College</a>, opened in 1974 as the second <a href="/wiki/United_World_College" class="mw-redirect" title="United World College">United World College</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Victoria,_British_Columbia" title="Victoria, British Columbia">Victoria, British Columbia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Pearson_Peacekeeping_Centre" class="mw-redirect" title="Pearson Peacekeeping Centre">Pearson Peacekeeping Centre</a>, established in 1994, is an independent not-for-profit institution providing research and training on all aspects of peace operations.</li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson_School_Board" title="Lester B. Pearson School Board">Lester B. Pearson School Board</a> is the largest <a href="/wiki/English-language" class="mw-redirect" title="English-language">English-language</a> <a href="/wiki/School_board" class="mw-redirect" title="School board">school board</a> in Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> The majority of the schools of the Lester B. Pearson School Board are located on the western half of the island of Montreal, while a few of its schools located off the island.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson_High_School_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Lester B. Pearson High School (disambiguation)">Lester B. Pearson High School</a> lists five so-named schools, in <a href="/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson_High_School_(Burlington)" title="Lester B. Pearson High School (Burlington)">Burlington</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson_High_School_(Calgary)" title="Lester B. Pearson High School (Calgary)">Calgary</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson_High_School_(Montreal)" title="Lester B. Pearson High School (Montreal)">Montreal</a>, <a href="/wiki/List_of_English_catholic_schools_in_Ottawa" class="mw-redirect" title="List of English catholic schools in Ottawa">Ottawa</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson_Collegiate_Institute" title="Lester B. Pearson Collegiate Institute">Toronto</a>.</li>
<li>There are Lester B. Pearson elementary schools in <a href="/wiki/Ajax,_Ontario" title="Ajax, Ontario">Ajax, Ontario</a>; <a href="/wiki/Aurora,_Ontario" title="Aurora, Ontario">Aurora, Ontario</a>; <a href="/wiki/Brampton,_Ontario" class="mw-redirect" title="Brampton, Ontario">Brampton, Ontario</a>; <a href="/wiki/London,_Ontario" title="London, Ontario">London, Ontario</a>; <a href="/wiki/Saskatoon,_Saskatchewan" class="mw-redirect" title="Saskatoon, Saskatchewan">Saskatoon, Saskatchewan</a>; <a href="/wiki/Waterloo,_Ontario" title="Waterloo, Ontario">Waterloo, Ontario</a> and <a href="/wiki/Wesleyville,_Newfoundland_and_Labrador" title="Wesleyville, Newfoundland and Labrador">Wesleyville, Newfoundland</a>.</li>
<li>Mike's Place, the Graduate Student Pub at Carleton University was named in 1973 in honour of Lester B. Pearson with permission of his estate.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The Lester B. Pearson International Student Scholarship is the most prestigious scholarship for international students at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Toronto" title="University of Toronto">University of Toronto</a>. It covers the tuition, books, incidental fees, and full residence support. <sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Civic_and_civil_infrastructure">Civic and civil infrastructure</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Civic and civil infrastructure">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tribute_to_Lester_Bowles_Pearson_(50218610328).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/Tribute_to_Lester_Bowles_Pearson_%2850218610328%29.jpg/220px-Tribute_to_Lester_Bowles_Pearson_%2850218610328%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="352" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/Tribute_to_Lester_Bowles_Pearson_%2850218610328%29.jpg/330px-Tribute_to_Lester_Bowles_Pearson_%2850218610328%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/Tribute_to_Lester_Bowles_Pearson_%2850218610328%29.jpg/440px-Tribute_to_Lester_Bowles_Pearson_%2850218610328%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2391" data-file-height="3825" /></a><figcaption>Tribute plaque to Lester Bowles Pearson</figcaption></figure>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Toronto_Pearson_International_Airport" title="Toronto Pearson International Airport">Toronto Pearson International Airport</a>, first opened in 1939 and re-christened with its current name in 1984, is <a href="/wiki/Canada%27s_busiest_airport" class="mw-redirect" title="Canada&#39;s busiest airport">Canada's busiest airport</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson_Building" title="Lester B. Pearson Building">Lester B. Pearson Building</a>, completed in 1973, is the headquarters for the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Foreign_Affairs_and_International_Trade" class="mw-redirect" title="Department of Foreign Affairs and International Trade">Department of Foreign Affairs and International Trade</a>, a tribute to his service as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_External_Affairs_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for External Affairs (Canada)">external affairs minister</a>.</li>
<li>Lester B. Pearson Civic Centre<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/Elliot_Lake" title="Elliot Lake">Elliot Lake</a>, Ontario was heavily damaged in February 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Lester B. Pearson Garden for Peace and Understanding, E.J. Pratt Library in the University of Toronto, completed in 2004 <sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Lester B. Pearson Place, completed in 2006, is a four-storey affordable housing building in Newtonbrook, Toronto, near his place of birth, and adjacent to Newtonbrook United Church.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Lester B. Pearson Park in St. Catharines, Ontario.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Pearson Avenue is located near <a href="/wiki/Ontario_Highway_407" title="Ontario Highway 407">Highway 407</a> and <a href="/wiki/Yonge_Street" title="Yonge Street">Yonge Street</a> in <a href="/wiki/Richmond_Hill,_Ontario" title="Richmond Hill, Ontario">Richmond Hill, Ontario</a>, Canada; less than five miles from his place of birth.</li>
<li>Pearson Way is an arterial access road located in a new subdivision in <a href="/wiki/Milton,_Ontario" title="Milton, Ontario">Milton, Ontario</a>; many ex-prime ministers are being honoured in this growing community, including Prime Ministers <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a> and <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Wilfrid Laurier</a>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pearson_Plaza" title="Pearson Plaza">Pearson Plaza</a>, a mall being developed in <a href="/wiki/Elliot_Lake" title="Elliot Lake">Elliot Lake</a> to replace the <a href="/wiki/Algo_Centre_Mall" title="Algo Centre Mall">Algo Centre Mall</a>.</li>
<li>Pearson Park, a playground built in 2013 in <a href="/wiki/Wesleyville,_Newfoundland_and_Labrador" title="Wesleyville, Newfoundland and Labrador">Wesleyville, Newfoundland</a>.</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sports">Sports</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Sports">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>The award for the best <a href="/wiki/National_Hockey_League" title="National Hockey League">National Hockey League</a> player as voted by members of the <a href="/wiki/National_Hockey_League_Players%27_Association" title="National Hockey League Players&#39; Association">National Hockey League Players' Association</a> (NHLPA) was known as the <i>Lester B. Pearson Award</i> from its inception in 1971 to 2010, when its name was changed to the <i><a href="/wiki/Ted_Lindsay_Award" title="Ted Lindsay Award">Ted Lindsay Award</a></i> to honour <a href="/wiki/Ted_Lindsay" title="Ted Lindsay">one of the union's pioneers</a>.</li>
<li>Pearson was inducted into the Sports Hall of Fame at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Toronto" title="University of Toronto">University of Toronto</a> in 1987.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Pearson was inducted into the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Baseball_Hall_of_Fame" title="Canadian Baseball Hall of Fame">Canadian Baseball Hall of Fame</a> in 1983.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Pearson_Cup" title="Pearson Cup">Pearson Cup</a> was a baseball competition between the <a href="/wiki/Toronto_Blue_Jays" title="Toronto Blue Jays">Toronto Blue Jays</a> and <a href="/wiki/Montreal_Expos" title="Montreal Expos">Montreal Expos</a>. Pearson also served as Expos' Honorary Club President from 1969 to 1972.</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Honorary_degrees">Honorary degrees</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Honorary degrees">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lester_B._Pearson_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Lester_B._Pearson_%28cropped%29.jpg/220px-Lester_B._Pearson_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="266" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4f/Lester_B._Pearson_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="283" data-file-height="342" /></a><figcaption>Lester B. <h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Electoral_record">Electoral record</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Lester_B._Pearson&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Electoral record">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Electoral_history_of_Lester_B._Pearson" title="Electoral history of Lester B. Pearson">Electoral history of Lester B. Pearson</a></div>

<dl><dt>Works by Pearson</dt></dl>
<p>Pearson published one memoir in his lifetime. The other two were written after his death by <a href="/wiki/Ghostwriters" class="mw-redirect" title="Ghostwriters">ghostwriters</a> and they lack the authenticity.<sup id="cite_ref-SYMPOSIUM_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SYMPOSIUM-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-extraordinary_38-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-extraordinary-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
